the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. it's about integrated border control. we all agree that the security of the european union begins at the external border. it is also where the first line of defence against organised crime, against terrorism, against smugglers, against illegal immigration begins. but we know that these external borders are still the weak link in the whole internal security system. the reasons for this are well known. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the schengen agreement is respected, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. but if we want to increase the security of the external borders and thus the security of the union, then we need equally high standards at all the external borders, at present and in the future, and we all know that the enlargement of the union is imminent, which means that action is needed here. the security we achieve is the prerequisite for the whole internal security complex, but also for the realisation of freedom of movement as a fundamental principle. i welcome the initiatives launched by the european council in laeken in december 2001, which instructed the commission and the council to take appropriate measures in this area. i welcome the commission's proposal and congratulate the commissioner on his excellent preparatory work, and i welcome the council's action plan. there is broad agreement here. i can also tell you - and i am pleased to say so - that there was a broad consensus in the committee on these proposals. i would therefore like to thank you for the close cooperation that has been established in the interests of security across all party lines. i think we have set a very good example here. i would be glad if there could be this broad support and consensus tomorrow. what are the main points of my report where there is agreement? i would like to highlight five of these priorities. firstly, we need to re-define the task and create a common set of rules. we also need to analyse the operational practice of implementing the schengen acquis. we know there are big differences here. the common border management manual should be recast with the common standards and taking into account best practices. it is particularly important to me that we not only have a common manual but also that we have a monitoring body which regularly checks whether the provisions are actually being implemented and complied with. this is what makes the common standards and the high quality. the second point is - and this is the basis for it - joint training and further training. we must finally implement article 7 of the schengen implementing rules. we are already in the process of drawing up a common curriculum for border guards. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this should be supported by the argo programme to ensure these training standards, but also the common operational standards in the long term. the third point is the compatible equipment. it is not possible to do so within the member states and it is not possible to achieve cross-border cooperation because the technical standards are totally incompatible. if we are to move towards joint deployment of satellite or radar systems or even cross-border cooperation, then compatible equipment is a prerequisite. it would also be useful - also for economic reasons - to have these standards in the future when jointly procuring technical systems - and these can be very costly. fourthly, we should be constantly carrying out a risk analysis. as with other systems of analysis at europol, when it comes to organised crime, we should have a risk analysis carried out by europol, the results of which are then communicated and made available to the individual member states: situation profiles, assistance to implement the common standards here. it would be desirable - and this would be a general objective in order to become more effective - that the exchange of data between the schengen, europol and eurodac systems also be carried out - of course in compliance with all the necessary data protection provisions. there is no doubt that some synergy could be achieved here. the fifth point is the establishment of a european border guard corps, composed of specialised units, financed by the community and, if necessary, acting as a temporary support for risky sections at the request of the member states, the national authorities. this would enable us to achieve both a high standard of security where there are particularly sensitive sections at our external borders and burden sharing. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's external borders are not affected by the crisis. we are thus achieving this aid and burden-sharing, but in principle it must be said that the general competence for the control of the external borders should remain in national hands. i hope that all this agreement will now give the necessary impetus and that we will finally make a reality of what we owe to the citizens - namely to offer them greater security, even in the event of enlargement. we can get to work. parliament will give its full support to the work of the commission and the council if it goes in this direction. (applause from the centre)

be able to support them. in the last few years, there has still not been all that is really necessary, namely, real coordination between the member states. mr jarzembowski has already pointed out that the railway sector is the only one in which the internal market has not yet been achieved. i would perhaps put it even more bluntly: in the case of the railway, we probably did not even realize it in the first place. the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. this has already been mentioned today. i can't stress it enough. the results are therefore poor, especially between the road and the railway, where the scissors are more likely to fall apart than to have come to meaningful joint solutions. the european railway system and its connection with the system of the candidate countries which will soon be members of this union must be revived and made functional, otherwise there will be many more failures on the railways and we will have even more problems than we have now. as an austrian, i would not only refer this to the brenner and the alpine transit, but basically to the european areas as a whole: all are more or less sensitive, not just those which we are discussing intensively these days. in this context, and by the way, a remark which is not uninteresting because it is unfortunately never taken into account in the debate: the austrian railways are the only ones which have increased their freight share in recent years. all other neighbouring countries have continued to give priority to the road. what is the specific issue in the context of the ainardi report, for which i was also the shadow rapporteur? interoperability should and must be achieved throughout the network. we cannot concentrate on the way we started, treating individual parts - high-speed rail and others - separately and individually. we must try to find a real structure, with the necessary exceptions, of course, for regional railways, which are real regional railways, for historical models and so on. in drawing up and adopting these technical specifications, we must bear in mind that this is not the task of the european legislator, but that it is the task of the specialists, and that european policy is only called upon to monitor. what is important in this context are reasonable costs and a high level of safety. both have been mentioned often enough here today. i don't need to stress this, but i think we should take it seriously. i would like to point out, particularly on the subject of safety, that we have now tabled a proposal which also envisages a black box system for the railways. this means that we also want to make progress in research to ensure that we arrive, in the long term, where we need to get: to a functioning european transport system. in writing. - (de) in principle, the commission's proposal for a regulation is welcome. most of the amendments proposed by the european parliament also complement it very well. however, i consider that the following points are problematic in relation to existing community rules and practice: the introduction of new higher standards using the concept of 'very significant damage' instead of 'significant damage' in the area of goods has the effect, through this new definition, of creating uncertainty, a possible weakening of our negotiating position on this point in the negotiations following the doha agreements and discrimination against service providers in relation to production companies (amendments 9, 25, 26, 38). the fact that companies linked by an alliance agreement do not have the possibility to bring a complaint leads, in practice, to a significant limitation of the possibility to bring a complaint due to the global interconnection of alliance systems (amendment 39). does amendment no 60 mean that a commission decision is deemed to have been adopted if no consensus can be reached in the council? in any case, amendment no 62 (qualified majority in the council) sets stricter standards for the introduction of measures in the area of services than in the area of goods. the determination of the community interest and the decision that measures will only be taken when the authorities are satisfied that their implementation serves the community interest, deviates from the fundamental principle that all measures must be taken in the community interest, except in exceptional cases. i hope that these points will be addressed in more detail during the procedure.
in writing. - (de) the proposals for the second railway package contain important initiatives to strengthen the european railway system. the acceleration of the opening of international services and the opening of the market for national rail freight services is an important step towards the liberalisation of the railway sector. in view of the objectives of the white paper on european transport policy for 2010 and the steady increase in european road traffic, the transfer of freight transport from road to rail must be considered as a top priority. a sustainable transfer of european freight flows can only be achieved successfully if the rail sector is both efficient and competitive. the planned harmonisation of the european rail network should therefore be welcomed insofar as a maximum level of safety can be guaranteed.

- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. i have to say, and this is particularly because of my predecessor, that we should not see this report as separate from other reports and initiatives, such as the lisbon strategy, which also aims at social cohesion and sustainability, the commission's report on the evaluation of national strategies for adequate and sustainable pensions, or the streamlining report on the coordination of employment policy with economic policy: all serve the purpose of maintaining the financiability of our social system, because it is necessary. demographic change affects all policy areas and has an impact on the social fabric and the socio-political framework. i therefore welcome this report and this debate as a contribution to raising awareness. there is a need for action by each of us and at every political level. according to a recent eurostat survey, fewer births but more deaths were recorded in the eu in 2002. the number of live births in the eu reached 3.99 million in 2002, 0.3% less than in 2001. in the eu, an estimated 3.68 million people died in 2002, some 60 000 more than in 2001. these figures alone show that we are facing a change in our society which has social, employment, financial and economic implications. i would just like to highlight one aspect, that of care. in my country alone - austria - there are currently 540,000 people in need of care and assistance. this figure will increase to almost 800 000 by 2011. at the same time, 84% of all people in need of care - and this is true for all countries - are cared for by close family members. only 26% need or receive or accept additional help. however, the number of family carers is decreasing due to demographic and economic changes. in the nursing sector, employment growth is 5.5%. the health and social sector is therefore a job creator. not all people can be employed because many areas of mobile nursing and care services are not sufficiently developed or there is not enough trained staff available. i therefore call in particular for the following: the upgrading and expansion of mobile services and the necessary additional services hospice, care of relatives, day care centres. we need to strengthen resources in the personal environment and to increase the support and guidance of family members. i believe that we also need uniform standards of vocational training and education, because the need for help often goes beyond national borders. we need an image campaign for nursing professions and an investigation into the causes of high turnover and short stay in nursing professions. we need european standards because care is one of the greatest challenges of our time. council following the european council in gothenburg, the eu's objective is to conclude the enlargement negotiations in time for the new member states to take part in the 2004 european parliament elections as members, and indeed this objective was achieved with the copenhagen decisions. the accession date of 1 may 2004 agreed in copenhagen achieves this objective by providing sufficient time for the ratification of the accession treaty, which was deemed necessary. the conclusions of the copenhagen european council indicate that commissioners from the new member states will join the current commission from the date of accession, i.e. 1 may 2004. following the appointment of a new president of the commission by the european council, the newly elected european parliament will approve the new commission, which should take office on 1 november 2004. on the same date the provisions of the treaty of nice concerning the commission and voting arrangements in the council will enter into force. the necessary consultations with the european parliament on these issues are ongoing and will be completed by the end of january 2003. these arrangements guarantee the full participation of the new member states in the union's institutional framework and the operational costs of such participation in the union's institutions are addressed through appropriate decision-making procedures.

mr president, the people of europe are overwhelmingly against a war in iraq, and there is no reason for this war at present except for access to iraq's oil reserves. preventive war is nothing but an active and unilateral attack. there is no justification for such a move without a un mandate. this war would have many losers. the u.s. and britain may be able to win the arms race. but there's a lot more at stake. the loser would be the civilized world, which would be shown how easily the international law that has been won over centuries can be replaced and abolished in favor of a preventive war of aggression.

international peace and security. in practice, this means that no austrian soldier will take part in war, but - as is austrian tradition - that we will take part in peacekeeping operations. it also means that overflights during wartime operations are not possible, but only in those areas expressly authorised by the security council, and that austria's sovereignty over land and air must ultimately be guaranteed. in this sense, we have fully supported the joint conclusions of the greek presidency of the council, as well as the - hopefully joint - resolution of the european parliament. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the commissioner is right when he says that this is a first attempt to regulate uniform rules for the legal immigration of self-employed persons and workers. i also welcome the proposals on many details, such as the 'natural' possibility for member states to limit the number of workers coming to the european union, the expulsion of criminals, the proof of financial means and so on. there are many positive things to be said, but there are two fundamental problems for us as the european people's party. in our opinion, the commission's proposal firstly exceeds the european union's competence in legislation. while the ec treaty provides for individual immigration regimes, the aim here is to establish a general regime throughout the european union. we want the main responsibility to remain with the member states and we want the european union to regulate what is its right, namely how to provide for the entry of third-country nationals into the european union, for example through a work visa and the corresponding controls, and then how to regulate freedom of movement within the european union. it is, and it should stay that way! the commission's approach to its proposal is also wrong in terms of content, because it assumes that there is a general need for immigration. the fact is that the situation in the various member states is quite different, both in terms of the qualifications of the labour force to be migrated and the level of immigration required. they derive a right to immigration, which is also subject to appeal. we have no such thing anywhere in the world, and we do not want to have it in the future! and if this proposal is then supplemented by proposals coming from the committee, then the report goes so far that we cannot agree to it. we want basic rules on accession to the union, but we do not want a general rule that largely displaces the competence of the member states!

and nothing has changed - there was 36 million tonnes of freight capacity available on the combustion axis alone. the railway is not being used. it is simply too expensive unless there is a reasonable toll system. a colleague thinks it's unfair to create conditions for austria alone. we are happy to show solidarity with the other regions of this continent. but it cannot be that only switzerland has the ability to levy a toll and also to invest the money in tunnels. we are also happy to be ready and i am absolutely in favour of building the brenner base tunnel earlier. but we must also have the possibility of funding. if on the one hand you do not create the possibility of financing and on the other hand you say that the railway must be built, but it is not even used when it is built because the appropriate incentive is not given, then the whole system cannot work. we very much hope that the road costs directive will come. i am curious about the debate and i see some in the chamber who will say that environmental costs must also be taken into account, that they are not that much, and that cross-financing must be very careful. i can already see the proposals for the next izquierdo collado report, which, although it is also committed to the principle of cross-financing, is not a good one. but the example of switzerland is rejected by the epp colleagues again, because it is too radical a consideration of the environment. i therefore believe that we should not just be talking about sustainable development, but also create the instruments. we know very well that an environmentally-oriented transport policy is one of the most essential instruments for creating sustainable development in europe. it is not therefore a special austrian regulation, but perhaps a pioneering role played by our country, and we do not want to give up this pioneering role until we have an alternative in hand. but we are prepared to advocate an alternative and to make full use of it. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. protocol no 9 to the act of accession of austria to the eu provides for a 60% permanent and sustainable reduction in emissions from transit vehicles between 1 january 1992 and 31 december 2003. this is a primary right between austria and the eu. neither the 60% reduction in pollutants nor the durability and sustainability have been achieved to date. this is also stated by the commission and the european environment agency in their technical report no 68. instead, there was a massive increase in the number of transit journeys and a 52% increase in pollution compared with 1992. the eco-points scheme expires at the end of 2003, although its objective of achieving a sustainable reduction in emissions has not been achieved. since the road-cost directive, which the european commission has often announced, is still not in place, a transitional solution has been agreed with austria from 2004 in the form of the transit succession scheme. this agreement must ensure that there is no further increase in emissions from transit traffic compared to 2003. the adoption of this report would have the opposite effect, namely a dramatic increase in emissions from transit. firstly, the report is a step down from the commission proposal, the danish council decision and the laeken council decision. secondly, the report ignores in its entirety the opinion of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, which was adopted by a large majority. thirdly, it is contrary to the objectives of the kyoto protocol and the alpine convention and to the environmental objectives of the european union in general. the european parliament's task is primarily to safeguard the interests of european citizens. if we do not do this, the people who are most affected will - as we have already announced - try to enforce their rights by blocking the roads. "never again war!" was a demand made after the first world war and also after the second. in order to put this sentence into practice, international legal norms have been developed which expressly include a clearly defined prohibition of violence. the primacy of force has been given to the united nations. it expressly rejects a pre-emptive war of aggression without the approval of the united nations. the american and british pre-emptive strike against iraq is a clear violation of international law. the consequences for the middle east, the arab world and the world community as a whole are incalculable. when one or more states take the primacy of force and apply it on their own, it is clearly the law of the strongest. fist law has a characteristic: it develops new fist law. in this context, the european union must do everything possible to prevent a unilateral war of aggression. the people of europe have long since understood this, now they only have to understand european politics!

- mr president, mr president of the commission! we have a very technocratic paper and a very political speech from you today, mr prodi. i don't want to hide the fact that i prefer the political speech. i would like to try to build a bridge between the two. in fact, mr prodi, you are right: the conditions are not favourable and are unlikely to be favourable in 2004. the threat of war in iraq has created new divisions within the european union and also within the candidate countries. today, unless things change radically, we are faced with a pile of foreign and security policy debris. and the economy - particularly in terms of jobs - has not been as positive as we expected. so we have to be a little more critical. first, the objective of enlargement: yes, it is right - we must see that enlargement leads to success. i would like to say to all those who today think - perhaps because of the events of the last few months - that we should stop enlargement, that this would only further divide europe and weaken it even more. but i admit that many of us are disappointed that the emotional and intellectual attachment to the european project among many of our neighbours - and indeed among some of our own members - is so weak. the european union, they are right, is not a supermarket, nor is the european union a bank where you simply collect money for some projects. it is not a subsidiary structure which is only to be invoked when unpleasant but necessary reform measures are to be taken. mr president, i think we have all probably failed to highlight the objectives which are the union's real raison d'être as a peace project, not just in the iraq question. the president mentioned the international criminal court. we have failed here too to convince the new members that the multilateral institutions, such as the international criminal court, but also the united nations, are very, very important to us and are close to our hearts. it was a mistake, it was a lapse. it may also be that we have sometimes taken a too legalistic and narrow view of the acquis communautaire. the commission proposes to create a circle of friends outside the enlarged union. but, mr prodi, in recent years we have not even managed to win enough friends in the governments of the candidate countries. and we must admit that the americans have sometimes been more successful - through lobbying funds, through a dense network of institutions and foundations, partly supported by the diaspora, and where necessary also through pressure and threats - in alienating at least the governments of some countries of the union even before they joined. it is also the commission's task to consider how to counteract this, how to promote the idea of europe more widely among the population, especially at the cultural and social level, in addition to the unified voice of the european union. now, as far as stability in and around europe is concerned: i think that we must look at the individual regions in a broader and deeper way and design the relationship accordingly. the objectives are not very precise and differentiated, because russia is to be treated differently from the mediterranean and the mediterranean differently from the balkan countries, which, moreover, have already been potential candidates since the zagreb summit, although this paper says that they could become so. but we have to admit that our visa policy for all these countries -- and i travel to these countries very often -- is shoddy. business people, scientists, journalists, students - all those who could help to make friends with these countries - are often pushed back by a very restrictive, rigid and bureaucratic visa policy. it's upsetting these people, and it's upsetting the most important opinion leaders in these countries. we must certainly have a restrictive visa policy, but it could be applied flexibly to these social groups. now to the third objective, sustainable growth. i admit it's a very vague term, but it's a term i can imagine a lot of things in. i just wonder if we really have a coherent growth policy. we are investing too little, too little in research and development, but also in infrastructure. let us think of public transport, which could also do more for the environment. too little is being done to make educational institutions more efficient and yet accessible to the general population. we often accuse people, our own citizens, of not being willing and courageous enough to take risks. but as social networks in our countries become more and more widespread and porous, it is no wonder that people are often afraid, afraid of risk. the tendency to ignore the provision of basic services - we are still waiting for a coherent policy from the european union, and especially from the commission, on the provision of basic services - often leads to people unfortunately sometimes taking a blind stand against globalisation, or at least distrusting the common market. i would like to emphasize your words again: yes, we are not a supermarket in the european union. we should be able to combine the objectives of sustainable growth with a policy of the social market economy. it is not an antiquated concept for my group to want more individuality, more flexibility, more mobility, more - to use a new word - employability: we will succeed only if people also have an adequate social safety net throughout europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we must therefore commit ourselves to a european model of capitalism, because it is more efficient, more social and more humane in the long term. i would like to conclude by saying that i welcome the paper, also on behalf of my group. but i think, mr president, that in the spirit of your speech, we need more political convictions and also strategies that respond to the psychology of the people in our own countries and in our neighbours. mr president, make sure that the commission's documents also make it clear that europe is not a technocratic or administrative project, but a political project. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i should like to begin by thanking three groups of people for this report, in particular the representatives of the spanish and always contributed by their behaviour to the fact that we are achieving this directive, but that we are not abandoning the principles we set out at first reading. i would like to thank you very much for that. the second point is that this directive has also clearly shown the problems we have in the legislative procedure. we received the commission proposal in october 2000, parliament completed its first reading in july 2001 and the common position was not sent to us until 5 november 2002. today we have the second reading debate and tomorrow the vote. i want to make it very clear here - and this must be remembered - that we wanted to set an example that we can conclude such an important directive with the first reading. the first reading of the parliament had 458 votes in favour. if the council had taken this step, we would have had this directive a long time ago. thirdly, this directive - and i say this because of some press reports today - is neither a pension nor a social product directive. it is not a directive on occupational retirement provision either. it is, as the name suggests, a directive on the activities and supervision of occupational pension schemes. i must mention this because it is a financial services directive, although i have of course received many requests for the definition of services, the design of products, etc. people ask, when they hear of occupational retirement provision: what is it to me, at a time when there is uncertainty in the pension field in all member states? we have tried to create in this tension - the financial services directive, the internal market directive, and the institutions playing a key role in financing the european economy and the functioning of the capital markets - not only competition but also social security in the same way, in the sense of lisbon. on the other hand, we were concerned with the design of occupational retirement provision as the second pillar and with the social aspects. this directive is a success of the european parliament. we have changed the commission's proposal at our first reading. we shall be changing the common position in our favour at the second reading. this directive is not a final product. she's a first step. i believe that this directive brings benefits to citizens because more providers are competing, that it brings more benefits to mobile citizens because the european market has been created, that it brings more benefits to citizens and providers because the second pillar is now also regulated at european level, that it brings benefits to the capital market because this directive moves and hopefully strengthens a financial volume of eur 2 trillion on the financial market. it is an advantage for the internal market because of the financing of risk capital and it is an advantage for the institutions because there is a european regulation and it facilitates cross-border activity. we have made changes in six main areas. the information for claimants has been extended. the biometric risks are clearly stated and coverage is provided. it was clearly defined that the lifetime pension is our goal. she should be the center of attention. the directive provides for coordination between the supervisory authorities. she has taken a first step towards a level playing field. it has not yet liberalized the investment rules. that was not possible with the council. it has not yet settled the question of tax harmonization. it has not yet achieved what we want, namely to make the second pillar uniform across europe and thus provide more social security. but it's an important step in the right direction. i think we have kept the balance within the limits of our competence and the possibilities that exist. i would like to thank all those who have contributed to this. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. if one were to read mrs buitenweg's report superficially and follow her in her presentation, one might think that what she is proposing here is actually quite unproblematic and could be accepted. she didn't do it unintelligently. i would like to warn you very strongly against actually following it. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in other words, it is already calling for an amendment to convention 61 and for cannabis to be removed from the list of prohibited products. i would therefore like to warn you in detail against following mrs buitenweg, because if you follow her, you are in favour of the release of drugs and against the health and future of our young people and our society. if you support mrs buitenweg, you are helping drug traffickers to extend their huge business to europe and you are damaging the programme set up by the united nations to help farmers to find alternative products and make profits from them, because you are opening up a new market for drugs in europe. and you end up promoting the spread of drugs instead of fighting them with all the vigour that should be our aim. the european people's party, the european democrats - and i say this as the spokesman of this group - are absolutely opposed to the legalization of cannabis. we are in favour of maintaining the un drug convention, on the contrary, we are in favour of the full implementation of this drug convention and not of its amendment. we are in favour of fully implementing the programme which the european union has set itself, namely to pursue simultaneously prevention, repression and reintegration. why are we for and why are we against the release of drugs? it is relatively simple: look at the dramatic situation in the european union, where one third of european young people have tried cannabis. almost 8% of 15 to 19-year-olds use cannabis daily, and 75% of young people consider cannabis to be safe, despite the fact that medicine clearly warns against this with several studies, because the use of cannabis poses a risk to the body and especially to the psyche and leads to psychological dependence. so there is no reason to advocate the liberalization of drugs and the liberalization of cannabis and the removal of cannabis from the list of prohibited substances. on the contrary, those who know the dangers of drugs for young people can only demand that there be zero tolerance for drugs! this is the demand that we are making in response, and we hope to get a majority for it in the vote tomorrow, because we are opposing an amendment to the drugs convention in order to protect young people and in the interests of young people. we also hope to obtain a majority of reason in the european parliament and then to take this majority of reason to the vienna conference to strengthen the un in the fight against drugs and to make it clear to it that we are in favour of maintaining the convention and that we will be fighting against any liberalization of drugs and against any legalization of drugs. we shall therefore certainly not be able to give our approval to the buitenweg report unless it is substantially amended. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. european surveys clearly show that the european citizen is moving further and further away from the common idea. one major reason for this is that many eu citizens have been misinformed in recent years. for example, 43% of europeans still believe that a work permit is necessary to work in any other member state. the advantages offered by the eu should be recognised. unfortunately, this is not always the case. this and many other examples clearly show that the eu's current information and communication policy is not working. the fact that the eu is not tackling corruption, nor is it effectively cutting down on bureaucracy - i am referring to our two parliamentary seats, for example - naturally reinforces this negative opinion among citizens. we should be a better form of government than the one citizens find at home. we must act urgently by the eu addressing its own shortcomings, removing them and putting together a complementary package of measures. for example, schools could introduce a specific eu subject and provide pupils with more opportunities to learn about eu affairs on the ground. these measures would be a step in the right direction to reduce negative prejudices about the eu and to improve the acceptance of a common idea among eu citizens.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. we are on the eve of the european union's greatest political project: the overcoming of the violent division of europe. we shall decide tomorrow on the enlargement of the european union to include ten new member states. despite this success of the european union, the march 2002 eurobarometer publication clearly shows that less than one third of the citizens surveyed consider themselves well informed about the european union. most indicators confirm that a broad group of 50% know nothing about or are indifferent to the european union. voter turnout has fallen from 63% in 1979 to 49% in 1999. there must be something wrong with that! i think one of our main problems is that we talk about the european union as a far away entity. that is what my predecessor said. the european union is all of us! i believe that the main cause of our problem is the distance in the minds and hearts between the people wherever they live and the european institutions. the political will to make european policy a domestic policy is lacking. the political will to reduce the distance between the citizens of europe and the european institutions is lacking, not in the european institutions but in the member states. or does every government in the member states say that there is no eu competence without the consent of the member states? is it being informed in the member states that there is no directive without the consent of the member states? is it openly stated that there can be no enlargement of the european union without the consent of the european parliament, the heads of state or government, the member states and the candidate countries? no, in doubt they say: 'those in brussels are doing this to us!' the citizen is not aware of the shared responsibility of the member states for all european decisions, because it is not lived and communicated. let me ask you a final question: are the regional parliaments fulfilling their role as parliamentary control of their representatives in the committee of the regions? i'm not going to. does the national parliament exercise parliamentary scrutiny over the council's activities? i'm not going to. does the european parliament sufficiently fulfil its role of parliamentary control of the commission and legislative work? not enough! if we do not fully fulfil our role as parliamentarians at regional, national and european level, the interrelationships and the interconnected decision-making mechanism cannot be presented in a transparent way. the best information campaigns are useless if citizens do not know that they are involved in every decision made by their governments. (applause from the centre)
the rapporteur. - mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, and especially my dear friends in the applicant countries, who are watching with interest our decision today. i have been involved in the enlargement process since the beginning of my work in the european parliament. i was able to help shape it in various capacities - as chairman of the joint parliamentary committee with poland, now as chairman of the committee with the czech republic and as rapporteur for malta. like all of us, i am witnessing a historic process that will change the eu for ever. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. it was therefore right that the european parliament, even before the council and the commission, was careful not to divide europe up again, to judge each candidate country individually according to its individual progress. i am pleased to be able to express today, together with the entire epc delegation, our support for the copenhagen outcome in the brok report, as well as for all the applicant countries. first of all, let me, as rapporteur, explain why i support malta's application for membership. the small island state is a country deeply influenced by european culture and history in an exposed geopolitical situation. like all candidate countries, malta has had to make significant political and economic choices to meet the copenhagen criteria. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the european union has been understanding and generous to malta, for example with transitional periods in sensitive areas such as shipyards, second homes, even with regard to requests for tax exemptions and, of course, the possibility of subsidies for agriculture. malta has recognised this opportunity. it was the first country to go to the referendum with an overwhelming turnout, voting 53% in favour of joining the eu. this referendum is not binding. the treaty of accession still needs to be ratified by parliament. the next elections on 12 april will decide whether there will be a parliamentary majority. i have always tried as rapporteur to avoid interfering in the domestic policy decisions of the country. i am still of the same opinion today and i do not want to prejudge the maltese people's choice in any way. let me just say this much: we in the european parliament have always supported malta's application for membership of the eu, we welcomed the fact that the application for membership, which had been suspended for two years, was renewed in september 1998 and that the helsinki european council decided to open accession negotiations with malta. it is a unique opportunity for malta. there will be no second chance of this kind. i very much hope that we will be able to welcome malta as a member of the eu on 1 may 2004. the enlargement of the eu to include the baltic states also has a northern, a central and a central european dimension. i am speaking as an austrian. the european union has a long history of cooperation with the countries of central and eastern europe. with enlargement, austria is moving from being on the periphery of the eu to being at the centre of an area from which we austrians in particular can expect economic impetus, growth and employment. the austrian people's party has always been committed to the european union and its enlargement. it is this enlargement which is being approved today by my delegation and at the athens summit by the austrian federal government. we are not only looking for economic benefits from this enlargement. i have always stressed this as chairman of the joint committees with poland and the czech republic. this enlargement is a reunification of europe. we expect, above all, good neighbourliness with those countries with which we have had historical links for centuries and from which we were separated as a result of two world wars and the cruel division of europe during the cold war. i do not think we can solve all the problems with our neighbours immediately when they join, but we can certainly solve them better and more easily when they join. i therefore call for the approval of all the candidates for accession and ask parliament to give it. it is an important historical act, an act which will help us to cope with the past and to make it easier to make the gestures we expect from our neighbour the czech republic. i am glad that we can expect such gestures, especially after the statements made by the czech deputy prime minister, mr mareš, yesterday in our group. it is with this in mind that i am, on behalf of my delegation, inviting and giving my approval to the applications for accession from ten candidate countries in the european parliament. (applause from the centre)
draft opinion of the committee on constitutional affairs. - mr president, the european parliament's committee on constitutional affairs welcomes the outcome of the negotiations with the ten applicant countries. we are pleased that this enlargement round will help to overcome the unfortunate legacy of conflict and division in europe, but we also point out that it is not over yet. we must not take for granted the approval of enlargement by the citizens of the candidate countries or the member states. we need a lot of information on this and we should be careful not to make propaganda. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are not left behind. in detail, there is a lot to add to these general remarks, particularly on the choice of 1 may 2004 as the date of accession. the committee on constitutional affairs welcomes the fact that we will have observers from the applicant countries at the european parliament's meetings by then. we welcome the fact that we will have new commissioners and new commissioners in total by 1 may. we also believe that the new commission should start work in november 2004. we do, however, deeply regret that all these decisions were taken without any consultation of the european parliament. we urge the council to inform and involve parliament early and in good time in the further course of the accession process. overall, we expect that many of the solutions which we had not really worked out on the basis of the treaty of nice will be made possible by the work of the european convention. we are pleased that the representatives of the candidate countries are also fully involved in this work. they will contribute to our consensus. the consensus will carry us on in europe. i have to say, madam president, rarely has a report come out in such a timely manner as that by general philippe morillon on the new european security and defence architecture: priorities and weaknesses, unfortunately more weaknesses. firstly, this report comes in time for the debate on the common foreign and security policy in the convention, which has been suspended until the end, and secondly, it comes at a time when the question is becoming more and more pressing in europe as to which direction a security and defence architecture should take: within nato or outside nato, as a partner of the united states in an intact transatlantic alliance, or detached from them as a european competitor in its infancy. the european union's foreign and security policy, which is aimed at preventing conflicts and strengthening international security, is in line with the principles of the united nations charter, and the establishment of a common foreign policy, which is the precondition for the development of a european defence policy, even if it is a matter of course, is controversial in this house, even though it has been clear since the kosovo war at the latest that the european union must be able to play a peacekeeping and peace-making role, as it is doing in macedonia. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i therefore think it is important that a supplementary motion on behalf of the epp-ed group calls for a permanent intervention force which must be able to operate in a kosovo-type conflict in the geographical area of europe by 2009, in cooperation with nato or autonomously. this is a deliberate attempt to avoid the debate on the direction of the eu. austria has made it clear since the ratification of the treaty of amsterdam that it wants to be part of a european security policy in solidarity. we welcome any step in a feasible direction and therefore support general morillon's concept.
madam president, i should like to begin by congratulating mr morillon on his report, which combines imagination and realism, vision and pragmatism in the usual balanced way. i would even wish that even mr korakas might read it once, because then he would come to a different opinion. the real question we face, of course, is, provocatively put, should the european union become a military power? i agree with my predecessor, mr laschet, that in recent weeks or months we have not played a role in world politics as europe, unfortunately! that's the fact. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. are we even willing to play a role? i think that the morillon report, which is adopted by a large majority, shows that we in this house have the will to play a sensible role in world politics. can a military fortification help us? i mean, yes, military support can help us if there is a common foreign and security policy. it cannot replace the common foreign and security policy, but it can help and support it. now i'm skeptical of an arms race. it would be grotesque to say that in the past we have been able to compete because we had an opponent to compete with. we must now arm ourselves because there is a partner, the united states of america, with whom we must arm ourselves. but - and i read it correctly - the morillon report says that the main, not the only, but the main point is to coordinate and coordinate our defence spending in such a way that we can achieve a much greater effect, a greater efficiency, with the same amount of money. the second is certainly to look at the gaps we as europeans can fill. in reality, there are two alternative models: i don't see it as an alternative to keeping things as they are. the alternative is that we become a military power like the united states, only a little less strong, a little less imperialist, a little less efficient. that is not acceptable to me. the other alternative is that we get military support for a common foreign and security policy, as morillon has said in his report, so that we are efficient and able to act within the framework of a multilateral policy. we're not currently. the morillon report shows ways in which we could act efficiently in the multilateral framework. that is why we support the morillon report unequivocally.

commission. - (es) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to thank mrs mcnally for her work as rapporteur on this decision. i know that you have worked hard with all your colleagues in parliament and in the council to get us to where we are. i think it is very important that today and tomorrow, with the votes, we can conclude once and for all the adoption of this programme. the present proposal for a decision of the european parliament and of the council concerns a multiannual action programme in the energy sector, which should cover precisely the period 2003-2006, and hence the urgency of concluding this debate. we have four specific areas: the rational use of energy, demand management, which used to be under the umbrella of the save programme; new and renewable energy sources, formerly called altener; energy-related transport aspects: steer; and the international promotion of renewable energy sources and energy efficiency, called coopener. before i discuss the amendments, i would like to make a brief statement on a subject which, as i have observed, has raised concerns in parliament: the coordination of the programme and its relationship with other community policies with an energy dimension, especially in the areas specific to the programme. that was just pointed out by mrs mcnally, who wants it to be clear and meridian. i therefore share the concern of the honourable members - and particularly the rapporteur, mrs mcnally - and in this respect i believe that inter-service consultation and cooperation is of great importance and should be a common practice. i have made a full commitment to increase and improve these interrelationships in the management of our programme. i am convinced that this will ensure that the energy measures are implemented in a manner consistent with the various community instruments. so, mrs mcnally, to that end i propose to set up an inter-service group, composed of all the commission services whose policies and programmes are related to this programme. i am talking mainly about the services responsible for development cooperation, agriculture, regional development, the environment and research and technological development. as regards the amendments tabled by the european parliament, i am pleased to see that, following the latest discussions with the council, a compromise has been reached on the amendments still pending. i would like to confirm and emphasize the commission's full support for the new text presented by parliament. in this connection, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to congratulate you on behalf of the commission on the agreement reached with the council, particularly as regards the budget for the programme, namely the famous eur 200 million, which is a considerable effort. most likely, mrs. mcnally - as we know - would want more. we too, but i think 200 million is a significant figure, which means, in four years, a very significant increase in budgets compared to what we have been using in previous times. i am confident that the vote recommended by the rapporteur will be approved and that rapid approval of the programme will allow it to be launched without losing the funds for 2003; that there will be overall approval by the end of the year, as we will have made sufficient progress on this, and that they can therefore be used, including the funds for 2003. i would like to thank mrs mcnally again for the excellent work she has done and the honourable members for their attention.

the commission has already made a number of proposals. the committee on legal affairs and the internal market has, in my view, provided a good basis. the council should follow parliament's example and decide and not delay the directive. the european parliament should adopt the basis for the decision and extend the directive to nuclear environmental damage. i think the directive is important because it makes the polluter pays principle a european principle and enforceable and because it strengthens the european regulatory model of the eco-social market economy. the austrian side and i, mrs flemming, have tabled 35 amendments. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i would like to highlight five points in particular: firstly, the directive will apply to those activities listed in annex i which have been clarified by our amendment. secondly, there is a disclaimer of liability for normal operation as authorized by the authorities and for emissions or activities which are not considered harmful according to the state of science and technology. this will lead to legal certainty. thirdly, the principle of shared responsibility has replaced solidarity and this has led to cost sharing in the case of several polluters. fourthly, the aim of the directive is to repair environmental damage and therefore the compensation for interim losses has been abolished. the committee also welcomes the commission's proposal to introduce a new system of taxation of capital, which would be compatible with the treaty. this is another way of striking a balance between different interests.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i too would like to congratulate mr corbett. it's not a simple matter. perhaps it can be explained - precisely because there are always visitors here at this time - most likely by the fact that we are dealing with a bridge-building exercise which the corbett report is now attempting to do to get away from this european confusion, the lack of competence or unclear clan competence, where suddenly, as my colleague mr radwan has pointed out, regulations are actually being made at a lower level which directly affect the lives of millions of people, without any direct democratic legitimacy for this. this has a lot to do with the history of the european union and the lack of a clear separation of powers that we are facing, so that much is lost in a blurry area, delegated and in some cases still, and in some areas even more strongly, civil lawmaking. the fact that the european parliament is defending itself is, i think, a historical merit and also a legitimation of this parliament. but what is certainly important now is that the convention, which we are currently studying very intensively, should be much clearer and even more precise in regulating what is allowed and what is not, and that of course includes the power of a european parliament, this form of popular representation, to take back such implementing powers after they have been delegated. it will certainly depend on that, but it will also depend on our being able to find, after the corbett decision, formulations and presentations which are actually comprehensible to the citizens and the voters, which bring clarity and transparency to the process and which make it impossible for anyone to hide his tricks. in this sense, i remain firm in my ceterum censeo, that there can be no democracy without transparency.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. in the constitutional convention - this has been raised several times today - we are discussing very intensively the abolition of the old comitology. i believe that there will be results in that direction. why are we doing this? that has also been mentioned. we are doing this because the old comitology essentially produced the underlying law in an undemocratic and, above all, non-transparent way. no one in the public knows what is being done, how it is being done, why it is being done. first of all, nobody in the public knows who has represented which positions here and why. we have to change that. we shall therefore abolish the old comitology in the constitutional convention, but - and this has already been mentioned - this is not possible without a substitute. we need delegated legislation, but we need delegated legislation with clear responsibilities, with clear rules of delegation, with clear mandates, and above all with the possibility of recall, and that is to say, the equal possibility of recall, both by the council and by parliament. i have already referred to our discussion in the convention on this subject on several occasions. we want the european people to understand who is exercising legislative powers and why, and the results that emerge from this will soon be available as convention law. but that does not make the work on the corbett report superfluous, quite the contrary. until we have written this result down at constitutional level and then implemented it, we need the findings of the corbett report to be as much in line with the direction discussed by the convention as possible. in this respect, we not only congratulate the rapporteur but also hope that the result will be accepted as unanimously as possible and that the old, undemocratic comitology will be replaced by a sensible reform of the way this important subject is dealt with in the coming years.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the liberalisation of the energy market is of course primarily to the advantage of consumers, of the citizens of europe. why is it an advantage? because they can buy the best performance for themselves in the market. they can differentiate not only in quantity, as in the past, but also in quality and, in future, in price. i think we have achieved something very great together here, namely with regard to labelling, that in future it will be mandatory for utilities to indicate where the electricity comes from, that in future this will also be independently verified by the commission, that we will have a group of european regulators exchanging experience among themselves and thus promoting competition for the benefit of consumers and european citizens. competition is performance, and that is what the citizens want, they want performance, they want freedom of choice. the energy mix must be shown on the invoice every year in future. this means that in the future, as a consumer, i will simply - at least as of 1 july 2007 - exercise the right to choose an electricity company that supplies 100% of its electricity from hydropower. i think this is a very big step forward for all of us, and of course, transparency will increase significantly in the future. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not distorted. finally, we attach particular importance to the full application of community law. we believe that the decommissioning funds, together with the interest generated by them, should be used only for their original purpose and thus also bear the costs of the type of electricity generation they cause.

used to the full if the principles of equality of all member states, balance between the institutions and the european parliament's power of attorney are taken into account. there must be no attempt speculation? it is pointless to answer that question. only now, there is a constellation that has a chance of succeeding. this is undoubtedly a credit to the european union and the road map it has credibility with the palestinians - too much sometimes for israeli terms - but this time, after the meeting between abu mazen, sharon and president bush, i still have hope that the new peace process can be kept out of the deadly cycle of violence and counter-violence. this has something to do with the whole political environment in the middle east. the fall of saddam hussein's regime - whatever one's attitude to the american intervention - has certainly broken a stone, has replaced a system that deprives the radical palestinian forces and those who want all or nothing of ground. iran, which has always supported hezbollah, should also play a constructive role and consider whether it wants to continue its support for this organisation as it has done so far. there is also a way to isolate radical forces now to give the moderates a chance. this is a strategy which can only work if the european union and the united states complement each other and do not pursue different strategies. the tragic and unsatisfactory situation in iraq means that there is now for the first time a chance that syria and possibly lebanon will enter into a peace process with israel, but that israel too must make sacrifices. the european union must not allow itself to be swayed by radical settlers and forces which believe that israel can only develop within biblical boundaries. the eu must also encourage him and support him. the majority of the israeli population is prepared to give up a lot in order to live in peace and security, but of course the palestinian side must also back off from the plaintive demand for the return of the refugees one by one, because that would mean the end of the state of israel. the european union must give serious thought to the question of whether it should be involved with its own peacekeeping force. i personally find it hard to imagine the security gains from such a force. firstly, because israel has never entrusted its security to any military force other than its own, and secondly, because such a force could itself become the target of suicide attacks without preventing them. in the end, it all comes down to president bush's determination to help the palestinians achieve statehood. he must demonstrate that determination in an election year. the road map will not be lost in the desert sands of the middle east unless the european union and the united states complement each other and act in unison. if they emerge as competitors in the middle east, it will create room for new radicalism. crisis centres, which are exposed to and are exposed to international terrorist networks and actions. but it is also true that the us and the eu perceive this threat differently and react to it differently. the united states considers itself to be at war with international terrorism and claims the right to launch pre-emptive strikes. they have done so in afghanistan, in iraq, and they are relying on military power which the european union has no match for. in these circumstances, the european presence and its definition of tasks is quite different. the europeans are neither able nor willing to open military fronts against international threats. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. just showing the give life to a common european policy and security strategy. the problem of european-american dualism and alienation between the united states and the european union can only have a negative impact on the search for a common european security strategy.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. last week, on friday, the convention adopted by consensus the draft eu constitution, which was drawn up by the representatives of the governments and national parliaments of 28 countries, the european parliament and the commission. many of us feel that it was a historic day. the aim of this draft and the completion of its work is to make europe more transparent, closer to the citizen, more efficient and more democratic. parliamentary democracy today is characterized by political parties that run for office and by members nominated by these parties, who are directly elected by the people, and who form political groups in parliaments. the convention strengthens the european parliament by extending the codecision procedure, full budgetary rights and the election of the president of the commission, taking into account the outcome of the european parliament elections. despite this reality, which we have been applauding for the last few days and are glad to have finally reached, we still do not have a legally binding statute for members of parliament because the council is blocking it. we have not yet had a regulation on the legal status and funding of european political parties. we do not have a uniform voting system. we have no way of giving mandates on european lists. the decision by the european parliament last week in strasbourg to adopt a statute for members of the european parliament has filled a gap. the decision on the legal status of european political parties, which will hopefully be taken tomorrow, will close a second democratic political gap, especially after the council's decision on monday, which we very much welcome. my group supports the motion and the compromise amendments because we have actively contributed to the closing of this democratic political gap. we are doing what amsterdam and nice have asked us to do. we are fulfilling our promise to the court of auditors and we are ending the cross-subsidisation that exists between political groups and parties. we are thus creating more transparency, openness and clarity in political work and in the financing of the activities of political parties. we are thus contributing to the europeanisation of national parties and the democratisation of work at european level. we are thus creating the conditions for european lists and for a uniform european electoral law. the epp welcomes the distinction between group work and party work, and the roles of national parties and european party work. we are pleased to see a clear distinction between european campaigns and national campaigns. we welcome the clear rules on the activities of donors. we welcome the clear criteria - qualitative and quantitative - and the necessary democratic legitimacy which must be met and given in order to be able to maintain the status of a european party. this is not discrimination against small groups, because a european party is only a european party if it is not only active at national level but has democratic legitimacy and allies in a certain number of member states. i would like to ask you all to close the democratic political gap that still exists tomorrow and thus ensure greater transparency and clarity. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would first like to apologize to mrs kauppi. she's sorry she can't be there by plane. she asked me to represent her. we know that this directive is an important part of the financial services action plan. what are the strategic objectives of this action plan for a financial internal market? the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods and services are respected. what's the story today? today we are talking about the prospectus to be published when securities are offered to the public or admitted to trading. what is a prospectus? a prospectus is a document published when shares or bonds are issued and contains information deemed necessary by the investor. what is the aim of the directive? the aim is to introduce a single passport for prospectuses, approved by the authorities of the country of origin and subsequently recognised throughout the union for the offer to the public and/or admission to trading on regulated markets of securities. the new directive will require only one authorization for 25 member states of the european union, instead of the 15 separate authorizations required despite two relevant directives. i would like to thank the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs from all the political groups very warmly for the time and effort they have devoted to this, and also the chairman of the committee. last wednesday we were actually further away than we were together at a teleconference, and then on thursday there was the possibility of preventing a conciliation committee, which i think is good and right, because we do not know whether the conciliation committee would have brought us further or perhaps even jeopardised this important directive. we can therefore say yes to the compromise because there have been six major successes of parliament. the first reading has already succeeded in introducing the sunset clause in the lamfalussy procedure, in improving the definition of professional investors and in giving greater consideration to small and mediumsized enterprises. in the second reading and in the last few days, it was possible to achieve the free choice of the supervisory authority for non-dividend securities with a minimum denomination of eur 1 000, which is de facto equivalent to a freedom of choice for the supervisory authorities for non-dividend securities. the commission has succeeded in reducing the approval periods, the delegation of supervisory powers is still possible and there will be a review after five years. it is only if the commission's report is negative after five years - which i do not expect - that the possibility of delegation must be withdrawn after eight years, so that we have also achieved an improvement in the common position in the direction desired by parliament. but that is not all. i regret that two things were not achieved. one concerns the basic prospectus regime for all credit institutions, which i have advocated. in this case, it was not possible to achieve the objective of the directive, which is stated in the recitals, namely to speed up the procedure for issuers which raise capital frequently. the directive has missed the mark here. the second point which was not achieved - although parliament decided to do so in an intergroup way - was the derogation from the directive for the issue of debt securities by credit institutions up to a maximum annual volume of eur 50 million per issuer. this would have been particularly helpful to countries such as austria and germany, because we do not need prospectuses at present below this limit. the limit is too low for parliament and we wanted to raise it to 100 million. i'm afraid that didn't work out. in sum, the compromise is a good one and we will support it.
