albeit laborious - attempt to improve the common position. the debate on the rejection of this common position is not, of course, off the table. i do not know whether there has not been a motion in this direction this evening. the temptation to do so is, of course, that the council has taken over a number of things from the european parliament in terms of quantity, but not in the harmless technical aspects, and unfortunately not in the major substantive aspects. i still consider it important that the two draft directives represent substantial progress compared with the current legal situation, including modernisation, simplification, the introduction of electronic procurement procedures or the use of electronic means in the procurement procedure, and facilitation for individual sectors. i am very pleased, for example, that the postal sector has been included in the sectoral directive, also on the initiative of this parliament. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the criteria for awarding contracts have been the subject of much debate today. i would like to clarify once again what has obviously not been made quite clear: the text now proposed in the common position goes backwards in relation to the current legal situation and deprives the european court of justice of the legal basis for very positive judgments. so we will be more restrictive in these award criteria in the future. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. one of the most important contracting authorities in europe is the local authority. mr bouwman has mentioned an example from his own parish. municipalities are directly democratically legitimised bodies, and if municipalities, for example, come to the conclusion that they want - even if it is a little more expensive - wooden windows from sustainable forestry, then they should be allowed to do so in the future. they are accountable to their citizens, they are accountable to their courts of account, and they are not swimming in too much money these days. that is my objection to mr karas. the first is to restore the present legal order and not to go backwards; secondly, it is not a question of mandatory criteria in the sense of 'every contracting authority must apply all these conceivable criteria', but of the possibility of doing so if it wishes to do so and makes it transparent. the current wording can only have the effect of restricting these possibilities in the binding part. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the european parliament. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. perhaps by comparing the voting lists tomorrow we can still rectify some of the legal committee. i can also announce that we will no longer support all the amendments that we supported in the legal affairs committee, in order to help us to go into the conciliation procedure with a little less amendments, which unfortunately seems inevitable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us. the greek presidency of the council fell on a hard time, and it was successful. i congratulate you on that, mr president-in-office. the european council has also shown that the principle of the six-monthly rotation of the presidency of the european council was a creative and enriching element, and the future permanent council presidency will have to prove this. the summit in chalkidiki was also a success. that wasn't part of the deal. the heads of state and government met, and almost every one of them had a long list of national ambitions which he wanted to snatch from the draft constitution of the convention at the last moment. but when our heads of state and government looked at each other, they were shocked and left the draft convention alone. it was a great success until the moment when it was decided to consider the laeken mandate fulfilled and to inform us - and i would like to come to that now, mr president of the council - that part iii only needs some more purely technical work. that's not what we're talking about, because why are we still working at the convention in july? because there was no consensus on the bureau's draft. hundreds and hundreds of amendments 1,700 and not on technical questions, but in essence this work is about a very clear question: whether we are also realizing the promises, the values and the objectives of the first and second parts, whether we are actually realizing the competences, responsibilities and principles of democracy that we have enshrined in consensus in the first and second parts, or whether we are only setting new objectives but not freeing the europe that is bound by the past. i would like to illustrate the dramatic nature of this situation with a few examples: the first part of the report is, of course, a comprehensive commitment to the principles of democracy and the rule of law. but in the third part there are still cases of council legislation alone on key issues, for example the european social order. national parliaments lose the right to ratify international treaties without this house receiving that right of ratification. in one of the most difficult cases, the lack of control by the european court of justice in the most fundamental area of internal police and judicial cooperation, 80% of the convention called for the derogations to be abolished. no politics without judicial control, no law without parliament - these are not technical questions, these are questions of self-determination and the definition of europe! the european parliament has been a key player in the process of european integration. we have been pressured by governments, the majority of the convention wants more, the citizens of europe want more. we know that the public in europe demands far more from europe than the governments want to give to europe. the convention has not yet reached the stage of making technical decisions, but it still has to make substantial reforms. public services of general interest, cornerstones of the european social order the vast majority of the convention calls for their explicit protection in the constitution. so far, that has been denied. i do not say this to diminish the work of the convention or the draft that has been put forward so far, but to draw the house's attention to the fact that much of what we have established in the first and second parts still needs to be achieved and that we will owe the people great promises if we do not make a real attempt at reform in the third part! mr president, mr president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen! the financial services action plan was pushed forward, the accession treaties were signed as a contribution to the reunification of europe, the balkan initiative was taken, the first two parts of the draft constitutional treaty were handed over, and yet in the last few weeks we have had to realise that in the context of the iraq war personal vanities and national egos were stronger than the objective of the common foreign policy and that we therefore had no role to play in the question of 'peace or war'. we succeed when we act together, we fail when we reduce europe to the level of intergovernmental cooperation. community action does not mean a national veto by unanimity, but increased participation by citizens through qualified, double majorities. the single market will only become the home market for 25 when everyone has the euro as their currency and when taxation matters relating to the single market are co-decisioned by parliament. the eu foreign minister is more than a proper and important functional title only when it comes to co-determination in foreign policy. europe is more than the sum of its parts. europe is more than the sum of its governments. we are all europe! the rapporteur. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the aim of this draft regulation is to ensure a high level of protection of human life and health, animal health and welfare, environmental concerns and consumer interests in relation to genetically modified food and feed. the high level of protection of human health and the environment is regulated by a very demanding authorisation procedure. i should like to say this at the outset, because many colleagues keep confusing health concerns with the limit value for unwanted and unavoidable pollution, which will be discussed later. for authorisation: the placing on the market of genetically modified food and feed is authorised only after an independent and rigorous assessment of the potential risks to human and animal health and the environment. the evaluation will in future be carried out by the european food safety authority, the authorisation by the commission. the european parliament called for greater decentralisation in its first reading. we've only partially won here. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the effects of the crisis. i am very pleased that the consumer-friendly labelling system for genetically modified food and feed has been adopted. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report, which has been very helpful in this respect. i was able - and i am very proud of that as rapporteur - to push through a very consumer-friendly regulation, even against the opposition of the largest group in this house, the epp. we also had a discussion about labelling gmo-free at this time last year. i was and still am strongly against it; the question is not raised at second reading, because it was already answered at first reading. i would just like to remind you that we know from many studies and surveys that a large proportion of european consumers want to know where genetically modified ingredients are. a major advance in this legislation - and this has been forgotten over and over again because of the other hotly debated points - is the first mandatory labelling of genetically modified feed, especially when one considers that a large proportion of genetically modified organisms imported into the european union are used as feed. one of the most hot topics in this discussion is the question of thresholds. i would like to make a few corrections at the outset. i know that this will not help, because the members who keep saying this have already written their speeches down. this threshold of 0.9% does not refer to the deliberate use of genetically modified organisms, saying 'only from 0.9% onwards will be labelled', but is a necessary threshold for technically unavoidable and accidental pollution. the european parliament has set the figure at 0.5% at its first reading and the council has now set it at 0.9%. the second reading was devoted to the question of coexistence, which is of course related to how to maintain the different forms of agriculture in europe in the future, but which is also directly linked to consumer choice. i wanted european legislation, which is logical; if you allow genetically modified organisms to coexist centrally, you have to also centrally regulate the rules for coexistence. that was not majority. the compromise which i have just seen, which i am very pleased with, which is supported by almost all the groups in this house and which is also shared by the other european institutions, is satisfactory. we are giving the member states the opportunity to take appropriate measures in this compromise to ensure coexistence in the future and thus consumer choice. i would have liked, as a member of the european parliament, to see an obligation on the part of the member states. unfortunately, it was not possible to negotiate this in the short time available. i believe that our compromise is a task for the member states not only to sit back and point the finger at brussels on this important issue, but also to live up to their own responsibilities and to protect the freedom of choice of consumers on this issue. i should like to take a few seconds to thank the other groups for their good cooperation.
i have to say, madam president, i shall give my approval to both reports tomorrow. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the labelling of foodstuffs is not restricted to the consumer. the council, in its common position, considers that the aim of the reports is to avoid misleading the consumer. how lovely! but it won't be like that! for accidental or technically unavoidable contaminants that may be present, labelling is not required, at least up to a certain threshold. i am grateful to mrs roth-behrendt for saying that so clearly and so beautifully. it is a simple matter of whether 0.9% or 0.5% or 0.1% or 0.01% is the right answer. if it's not dangerous to your health, and it's not, then why all this absurd theater? poor consumer! he'll eat gmo, but he won't know it. he's being deliberately misled by us. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the labelling of genetically modified organisms is not carried out in the member states. the good man, he has made such an effort, but we are not going to label and inform the consumers! he'll never know what he ate. he has no idea he's been eating genetically engineered food. i tried to prevent it in committee and i campaigned for a 'closure with the negative label' and 'yes to a positive label'. it would have been honest, and big companies like nestlé, unilever, etc., would have gone along. i guess it was too late. let's take this as a lesson. let us rejoice that we have achieved coexistence, let us hope for a corresponding accountability, and let us take it as a lesson in what political dishonesty can be!

decisions on verifiable facts. in 1996 we noticed that our air quality measurements did not decrease due to high levels of nitrogen oxide pollutants at the measuring points, despite stricter vehicle limitation requirements and emission models predicting a decrease of about 45%. due to these specific high emissions from heavy-duty vehicles, it was suspected that the increased emissions could come from this sector. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the accuracy of the results has been significantly improved and the results have also been published in an international study involving the best measurement organisations in europe. and i quote from that final report: it is noted that the emissions of heavy-duty vehicles, especially with regard to the pollutant group nox, have been significantly underestimated. this concerns vehicles equipped with engines of the euro 2 and euro 3 limit values. the vehicles have significantly higher emissions in real driving than the limit values suggest. the reason for this is to be found in the targeted programming of the engine control of the vehicles. vehicles are designed to have lower emission levels at the measurement points that are covered by the statutory type-examination cycles. in those operating points which are not tested in the type-test cycle but are of great importance in driving practice, the vehicles have the same or even higher emissions compared to older engines. other the regulation of eco-points is also aimed at reducing nox emissions and i have never claimed that the regulation of eco-points is the icing on the cake. this study now shows us that it can be stated that there is no technical basis for the reduction of the number of eco-points recorded per transit journey for euro 2 and euro 3 compared to euro 1. when i asked mr caveri to include these facts, he referred to his amendment no 17, which says, in a very i have to say, madam president, the report is not about creating conflict, but about harmonization. and it's not about equality, it's about equal treatment. and that, i think, is a very big difference that you should remember. gender budgeting is not about creating separate budgets for men and women, but about ensuring that resources are distributed fairly and efficiently. public budgets are not gender neutral as is very often heard, they affect men and women differently in terms of both income and expenditure. we want to highlight and take account of these differences. the economic, social and societal impact of budgetary decisions on women is not well known. that is why we need a budget analysis. analysis means looking at the effects of households on women, on the tax systems, the different types of taxes. how do women take up saving, and how are they affected? very often more than men. for gender budgeting to be possible, we need transparency, voice and decision-making. a group of very committed austrian women have written a book entitled 'women, power, budgets' and 'gender-based public finance'. i can recommend this to you as a basis, and it can also serve as a basis for the information brochure which the commission has promised us. this information brochure should be published everywhere, be accessible and be accompanied by an information campaign. then we can disregard the remarks we have just heard. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. if basel ii, as developed by the basel committee, is implemented, access to credit will be facilitated for those firms which do not need money, i.e. credit. on the other hand, access to credit will be drastically reduced for those firms which need credit, i.e. money. in austria, for example, about 65% of the enterprises are financed by bank loans. the eu average is only 46%. these enterprises are usually weakly endowed with equity capital. the construction, tourism and catering sectors are particularly affected. the equity ratio of the catering industry in austria, for example, is only 1.75%. however, since the equity ratio is the key factor in future lending, these firms, i.e. these sectors, would receive no or little credit under basel ii. the consequences would be catastrophic. the factories could no longer invest and modernize and would have to close. we would lose thousands, if not thousands of factories and jobs. i can only agree with the rapporteur when he calls for an analysis of these effects so that the negative consequences of basel ii can be exposed and at the same time countermeasures can be taken to prevent them.

adopted by the commission on 16 december. the commission is not the dependence on oil in the eu is currently 75% - we have seen that. if we continue with our policy of uncontrolled burning of oil, this dependence will increase to 95% by 2020. the risks and dangers of this dependence, as well as the economic and environmental consequences, could be the subject of a thousand-page treatise. the world's oil production is expected to reach its peak soon. the various forecasts made by partisan institutions, which are essentially contracted by oil interests, are to be met with the greatest suspicion. it is recognised that the global availability of oil has been declining since 2000. it is therefore generally to be welcomed that the idea of solidarity in supply, but also the idea of security of supply in general, is beginning to take hold in the eu. the problem is, however, to be seen in the medium and long term. without the development of alternative fuels and alternative drive technologies and a change in the whole mobility behaviour, the problem will not be solvable. the eu must develop autonomous technologies for the use of bioenergy fuels based on renewable primary energy sources. in parallel, the 18% energy saving potential must be exploited. it would certainly be useful to convert the non-binding objectives into binding ones. the amendment which proposes that the price of oil be fixed in euros in future is quite interesting, but we must still think hard about what this means in the context of world economic policy. i think that's probably not going to be a very easy way at all. natural gas consumption is also increasing strongly in the eu. when the oil resource becomes scarce, i.e. no longer available in sufficient quantities, the demand for natural gas will increase by leaps and bounds. however, anyone who thinks that natural gas can easily replace oil as a resource is dangerously mistaken. the transition from oil to gas is not an energy policy solution. the development of renewable biogas has made great strides in recent years. this has not been sufficiently recorded. as the technologies are relatively new, and precisely because they are new, they need massive support from eu research funding. biogas is a storable, renewable energy source and therefore of particular strategic use and value. biogas can be produced locally from biogenic materials, such as grass, biological waste and wood. we are still assuming a relatively low price for gas and oil, but i believe that with the shortages looming - and the price of oil and gas having tripled in recent years - the competitiveness of locally produced renewable energy sources will improve significantly. on security of supply: i think that in the overall context it is a relatively immaterial question whether the eu sets its oil stocks at 90 or 120 days. the argument that cost reasons are opposed to a 120-day supply is open to debate. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. even 120 days would provide only minimal relief in a real shortage. here too, it is important to emphasise the need to develop scenarios that reduce overall oil and gas consumption and promote substitutes based on sustainable production. i would like to conclude by pointing out that the price of energy may change dramatically in the next few years - i am convinced of this, and this has also been the case in recent years - due to the reduced availability and also to the increasing instability in the oil-producing countries. we are seeing this in the middle east at the moment, we are seeing this in a potential crisis centre in central asia, we are also seeing this in africa, and so i welcome all three of the comments as a thought-provoking principle. it is a thought - whether right or wrong, i do not think it is so crucial - from which we must develop something, namely a long-term energy accession countries on the way to being able to fully adopt the single currency euro. the second cornerstone for us is the independence of the ecb. the independence must be expressed by continuing the are a group that clearly says yes to the single currency. the single currency is the basis for a functioning internal market and the extension of the internal market to the domestic market. we also use these criteria to evaluate the candidate. in both the written and oral submissions, the candidate has unequivocally expressed the principles of the stability and growth pact, price stability, independence and the single currency, and has rejected all attempts at sabotage as a weakening of a stable currency, price stability and a policy for growth and employment. asked about the main principles, trichet said: 'the maximum degree of credibility in pursuing the objective of price stability, the independence required by the ec treaty from any kind of power or interest group, and finally realism and pragmatism in taking account of the changes brought about by the influence of new technologies on the behaviour of economic actors.' secondly, he said: 'price stability is indeed a necessary condition for sustainable growth and the creation of lasting jobs'. he stressed that price stability was not enough and had to be complemented by a sound budgetary policy, by a combination of productivity gains and moderation in normal wage developments, and by urgently needed structural reforms, and pointed out that the stability and growth pact provided the regulatory framework and common rules for the different economic policies of the member states. in each of his replies he has therefore unequivocally confirmed our principles as being correct. that is why my group will support it. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would like to begin by thanking all those involved in this compromise, starting with the rapporteur and alexander radwan. it was a difficult job, but we succeeded. i would also like to make it clear that the european parliament intends to adopt this directive during this parliamentary term, and we hope that the compromise will also help the council to find a compromise. the new investment services directive will strengthen the european internal financial market. why do we need a policy at all? because insufficiently harmonised rules, high bureaucratic burdens and outdated investor protection provisions have so far represented serious barriers to crossborder securities trading. what are the main points of our compromise and the new rules? firstly, the protection of investors. investors must be well informed and the code of conduct is one of the main pillars of investor protection. secondly, differences between the consulting and non-consulting trades. in future, only consulting will require a proficiency test. thirdly, we want the best possible execution of the service. the committee has found a formulation that can be accepted by both smaller and larger investment firms. fourthly, the crucial point: the level playing field between banks, which trade securities in house, and stock exchanges. the compromise now achieved is that banks are subject to appropriate supervisory and transparency rules. the aim is to allow internalisation schemes but not to operate at the expense of the exchanges and other market participants. we hope that this compromise will allow for fair competition between banks and stock exchanges. consumers would benefit most from this. i ask everyone to give their consent to the compromise the day after tomorrow.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i hope that this parliament will today support the draft convention by a large majority. there is a great deal of agreement in this house that the best thing about the convention was that it was not an intergovernmental conference. it was parliamentary-dominated, it was public, it was in dialogue with citizens and civil society, it had many months to work. the worst part of the convention was that it ended up being a governmental conference. negotiations and backroom bargaining, national self-interest against european veto threats, determined the outcome. when the convent was free, it was successful. it followed a constitutional logic, it took a design attitude towards the future of europe. the results were the draft of a european democracy, the charter of fundamental rights, a republican basic order for europe, the capacity to act and the possibility of a new role in the world. if the convention bowed to the pressure of governments and got lost in its own self-interest, it was unsuccessful. we have no european social contract, no european social order, too few majority decisions and still council laws, etc. the question then was not what we can do for europe, but what we can withhold from europe. now many governments are calling for a 'real' intergovernmental conference. this is a dangerous threat with a highly precarious legitimacy. ladies and gentlemen, plus and minus is a minus in mathematics. what is the point of imposing a successful method on the convention on a chronically unsuccessful method on the intergovernmental conference? the end, the result, will be, according to this simple mathematics, the result of an intergovernmental conference, a nice ii. not the greatest common denominator, but the smallest. and the governments, they weren't represented at the convention? highly ranked? did they not force their point of view? commissioner barnier, weren't you at the convent? didn't you agree to a big compromise? the commission is not prepared to accept the historic responsibility of opening the door to governments to unilaterally change this historic consensus in the name of their own interests. doesn't that make you a waterer of destructive forces? your place, commissioner, is on the side of the european parliament, which is fighting for this compromise. you will have to answer for that, and that is perhaps the gravest mistake the commission has to answer for in its time in office. what can the intergovernmental conference do without destroying this consensus, without continuing behind closed doors the old nice bargaining? it should accept the draft convention, it should deal with those issues which it has blocked in the convention, on which there is no consensus, in the spirit of the majority of the convention, especially the revision clause. the convention has not reached a consensus on this point; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budgetary authority is able to meet the requirements of the budget. it was also intended to resolve the contradictions between part 1 and part 3, which were mainly due to government intervention. if we promise people the social market economy in part one and the free, unrestrained competition in part three, then that is dishonest to the citizens. if we promise full employment in part one and only high employment in part three, that is a trick that will not strengthen confidence in europe. mr president, if we make the wrong decision at this historic moment, we will be leading europe into a crisis. we have nothing left if we destroy and discredit the convention's method after the method of the intergovernmental conference. what then will we have in the crisis? so, as difficult as this compromise has been for all of us, we should defend the convention on the future of europe. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. there is no doubt that the eu had to respond to the enormous increase in patent applications in this area. but it must also be clear that these cannot be directed against the interests of small software companies. the commission proposal, however, encourages monopolization of the software market, as small software companies are unable to afford to litigate over patent rights. the increased patent protection would promote the strong market position of the large providers and would also mean that software developers would have to pay fees, i.e. licence fees, for a wide range of standard functions. in the end, it is the consumer who will pay the price. the intention to create more legal certainty with this directive is welcome, but it is not the case here. on the one hand, there is no uniform definition of the technical contribution necessary for patentability, and on the other hand, the boundaries between patentable end products and non-patentable pure software are blurred. the eu needs a patent law that promotes innovation but also better protects financially weak inventors. both are not guaranteed; i am not saying that the commission should be unable to do so.

mr president, mr president of the commission, mr commissioner! i think this debate reminds me a little of the debate we are having on services of general interest, the so-called public service, and competition law. although the professions are not a supermarket - and a supermarket is not a service of general interest in the sense of a social provision - there is something in common. in the area of social security, we agreed that a framework for exceptions would be established on the basis of competition law, on the basis of state aid law, on the basis of the internal market, which could be interpreted and defined in a subsidiary way in the light of the specific features of different identities and cultures. i believe that the basic principle of subsidiarity, the consideration of different cultures and identities and the situations that have developed historically, can also be applied here. but we must also be correct. we are supporters of competition law - not just commissioner monti's. i therefore believe that it is appropriate and desirable to apply eu competition rules to the freelance sector as long as the specific features of the market are taken into account. i therefore expressly welcome the fact that the commission does not question the existence of professional associations. but now we've reached the crucial point: the free professions such as lawyers, notaries, engineers, architects, doctors and accountants have a very special character, a very special historical development. they require a high level of qualification and a special relationship of trust with their clients. however, they are also characterised by the fact that the consumer does not have the same information available to him as the service providers. and we must take these particularities into account. i therefore believe that the commission must not make the mistake of interpreting the rules of origin as an obstacle to the freedom to provide services. i am, however, fully in favour of the commission's view that self-regulation should not isolate the market and should not be detrimental to consumers. yet, you cannot comb everything. the main objective of these regulations is to guarantee the quality of services through ethical objectives and to prevent conflicts of interest and misleading advertising. standing rules are introduced by the freelance groups under their own responsibility to ensure the quality of the services. special standards are established to ensure that these rules are observed in a professional manner and to guarantee professional ethics. standards are necessary in my view to ensure the impartiality, competence, integrity and accountability of the members of the professional bodies concerned and to protect the customer. the parliament and my group stand with the professions on the basis of their ethical quality, their security vis-à-vis the customer, their competence, their impartiality and integrity, and we ask that this special status be given due consideration and security in the context of competition law. deficit at olaf, which has been brought to light in a very dramatic way by the eurostat case. this is not the time to blame anyone, but if we have to say that even with a generous figure, of the 300 staff of olaf, not even 15 are currently involved in internal investigations within the institutions, then something has gone very wrong. then this office, as the bitch said, has missed the point. we need to change course quickly and radically. olaf must concentrate its investigations on those areas where the competent services of the member states cannot or will not intervene, i.e. internal investigations within the institutions and on expenditure managed directly by the commission. secondly, respect for the fundamental rights of the persons concerned by investigations. the olaf regulation is quite clear on this. i quote: 'the office's investigations must fully respect human rights and fundamental freedoms, in particular the principle of fairness, the right of the persons concerned to express their views on the facts affecting them and the principle that conclusions drawn from an investigation may be based only on facts capable of being proved.' we have heard from the reports and opinions of the olaf monitoring committee and from the reports of the ombudsman that this is the most dangerous area at the moment. the regulation is simply and flagrantly ignored by the office's investigators in this respect. i would just like to recall the most recent example, the criticism made by the ombudsman last week against olaf in connection with the defamation of a journalist in public statements made by the office. if the fundamental rights of the persons concerned are not respected, then the results of the investigations will be worthless in the end, and i fear that olaf will therefore be surprised, particularly in the context of eurostat. what is lacking is an effective and, above all, preventive control mechanism to enforce compliance with the provisions of the regulation. this is a point which we must counteract by amending the regulation itself. we don't want to undermine the decision-making powers of the director, but we need a stronger counterweight. this counterbalance is the olaf monitoring committee, but under three conditions. firstly, the amendment in question expressly empowers the commissioner to ensure that the fundamental freedoms and rights of those concerned by investigations are respected. secondly, he will be given the additional resources to carry out this task and, thirdly, last but not least, his independence from the director of olaf will be strengthened by strengthening the committee's secretariat not only in terms of staff but also in terms of organisation, since it will no longer be based at olaf or the commission. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the control of the use of the financial system by the member states of the european communities for the purpose of the prevention of fraud is a very good one. ladies and gentlemen, i believe that we have done our work well in committee, that we have a responsibility to our constituents and to the taxpayers of this union, and that is why i recommend that this house should adopt this report. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. a debate on lifting the arms embargo against china is, of course, designed to be a woodcut argument and discussion. i will try to put some nuance into it, because i think some of my colleagues are making it too easy. i would like to say at the outset that i do not think it is reasonable or wise to lift the arms embargo. it is not a question for me that i am against lifting the arms embargo. however, we must look at china in a more nuanced way than we have done so far, and i am exceptionally closer to mr tannock than other colleagues who have spoken today. china is a large, let's face it, capitalist economy with a one-party system superimposed on it. it is trying to achieve for its people that they can participate in the prosperity that is developing in the world. the greatest danger is whether china can maintain the political structure of a common state and satisfy the social needs of its people. because the biggest problem for china is not the weapons, but the increasing hunger and poverty in parts of the population. china certainly has the big problem that there are different cultures, languages and ethnicities in the country, and it has not yet managed - and by the way, not only china - to give these different cultures and ethnicities the space that is necessary to maintain the political unity of the country. that is why china does not need our arms, but our sympathy and our support in this difficult development process. i would not wish anyone in this house to be a major responsible person in this difficult great state of china. i think that is why the resolution is somewhat wrong in treating the taiwan question only in the admittedly correct sense that china should not develop a threat scenario. i think that president bush - i am closer to him than daniel cohn-bendit, otherwise i am not - is right when he also warns taiwan against taking unreasonable steps. i'm not talking about whether they're legitimate, morally justified or not. in times of crisis, there are also steps which may be objectively and abstractly justified but which can nevertheless contribute to promoting a warlike or crisis-like development. we should not only warn one side from this parliament against taking unilateral and dangerous steps, but also say to the other side: 'be reasonable, restrain yourselves! try to leave the way open for a peaceful settlement! i think that is an important message which is unfortunately missing from this resolution. so my final word is no to the lifting of the arms embargo. china needs our full help and support, but not in the arms sector. both sides in this crisis, china and taiwan, should be told: 'stop your footsteps! we are in a time when peaceful rather than war solutions must be found.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. in europe we have been talking for more than 20 years about the so-called new approach. we now have a different slogan: the european union should no longer regulate everything and everyone in every detail, but should concentrate above all on the interoperability of systems. but unfortunately - and this is the problem here - we often fail to follow this principle. but not in this directive, and that has been criticised on several occasions. the commission has proposed a forward-looking system - admittedly with a long deadline - but as the commissioner has said, it is the only one that will not work. because that's not what we need. we need systems that work - plural - that exist, that work, and that are interoperable. i'm all for satellite technology, but not like this. is the bankruptcy in germany not enough for us? technology neutrality is what we need here and now and will need for a number of years to come. in this context, we must thank the rapporteur, renate sommer, for having steered the debate in the committee in the direction we need very early and in good time, namely that we should focus on ensuring interoperability, that we should focus on making things easier for the user, and that we should focus on making things work. in this connection, i should like to thank mr turmes, who has rightly warned us against losing ground on the subject of tolls and toll systems. when he started, it almost sounded as if he were talking about the next item on the agenda, the eco-points. but i can agree with him on this point on the agenda and also with what he said on this point for discussion now: we should not lose ground and we should make sure that we achieve functioning systems for our transport system as a whole, otherwise we will be destroying more than we are organising better.

prevent a mediation. we could have put some more things in there, but i find that the main points of our rough direction of purpose have already been fulfilled. we wanted to make sure that there was a directive because, of course, it is very important for next year, when the internal market will be outside the households, to have a level playing field where market participants have the same basic conditions. that is why the definition in this area was very important. we are here to strengthen the security of energy supply. the first is, of course, the micro-chp. we want to have a lot of market players that are improving the security of energy supply, but also improving energy efficiency, and above all -- i think that was one of the really compelling reasons -- to improve grid security. what we've all learned from the blackouts is that the electricity traded and the electricity actually flowing are often very different, and that's why we need a lot of points in the grid to ensure that we have grid stability. in this case, households will also have the opportunity to recover part of their energy costs through electricity supplies. micro-chp is an opportunity for households in the energy sector, including the private market, to obtain energy efficiently and cheaply while increasing energy security. we have some of that in the economic sector, but also in the budgetary sector, and we are also here to work out benefits for our citizens. i believe that a reduced energy bill will eventually be felt in the pockets of every citizen. the uniform definition of chp is very important. i think the most important point is that we have come to accept the various methods of calculation, in particular the proposal from cen, which we are very much looking forward to. we have pro thermo as a basis, but the net electricity calculation we have proposed should also be seriously considered by the commission. we have set a standard here which will be very important for all in the future. the balance between the self-sufficiency of the industry from cogeneration plants and the supply of energy to the grid is also an essential basis for us, and the balance between mechanical plants and those which do not supply the grid is very important. i think we've achieved an optimal result here. none of us has reached the maximum, but it is precisely through this optimum that we can show that europe is important for our elections next year.

draft opinion of the consultative committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us in the past. it is now almost six months since the question of coexistence also played a very important role in this house. at that time, the subject was the regulation on genetically modified food and feed, for which i was rapporteur, and we had to fight a hard battle against the commission and against some member states to win this room for manoeuvre, namely article 26a. we have succeeded in doing so, and i congratulate mr graefe zu baringdorf on the fact that we are sending another important political signal from the european parliament on this issue with his own-initiative report. i think it is more than important to call on the member states now to use this margin of manoeuvre which we have won for them in the european parliament to give it life, but i agree with my colleague graefe zu baringdorf that it will be necessary to achieve european rules on coexistence. i cannot understand the commission saying that we are able to grant centrally - we have the competence - but we do not have the answers when it comes to the questions of coexistence. i think that the own-initiative report by mr graefe zu baringdorf is also important because the seeds directive will soon be voted on by comitology. the european parliament is unfortunately not involved in this. i also think that the part where we say that the contamination value of seeds is set at the limit of detectability is extremely important. we have to be clear: the threshold of 0.9% that we have decided on is extremely difficult to meet if we do not insist on absolute purity for seeds. i hope that we can send this important political signal from this house tomorrow.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet adopted a decision on the matter. i would first like to thank the rapporteur, mr graefe zu baringdorf, for taking the initiative to draw up this own-initiative report on coexistence and for bringing together all the different opinions in this report. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. one thing must be clear: we are no longer discussing the question of whether genetic engineering is a yes or a no. that is what the rapporteur said. that question has been decided long ago. we must look ahead and start discussing how the coexistence of genetically modified crops and non-genetically modified crops can work in such a way that all parties can coexist without any disadvantage to anyone. this coexistence is intended to ensure freedom of choice for farmers and consumers, and in the interests of all european farmers, clear rules must be established to allow the smooth coexistence of different forms of farming on the basis of the farmers' voluntary choice of the type of farming chosen. we have reached a compromise in the committee on agriculture and rural development after intensive discussions both within the groups and between the groups and we have voted on a report which contains clear and conclusive demands. our own-initiative report calls for immediate arrangements at community level, because the guidelines proposed by the commission are a collection of non-binding proposals. we therefore expect the commission to ensure that a common policy such as the agricultural policy also provides a uniform framework. the commission has already made a number of proposals in the past, and i am sure that the commission will make a number of proposals in the future. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the pollution of the environment. the question of liability must not be carried on the backs of our farmers under any circumstances. in addition, it is necessary to establish the presence of gmo contaminants in seeds at the technically measurable and reliable detection limit in order to ensure that agricultural production complies with the applicable 0,9% labelling limit for food. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. an own-initiative report is a clear political signal from the european parliament. if we now soften the demands and statements of the committee on agriculture and re-launch our package, we will no longer be making clear demands. it is a collection of vague statements without clear guidelines. and we have to ask ourselves as members of the european parliament whether we really want to send a political signal which is not really a political signal at all. i therefore hope, as does the rapporteur, that this own-initiative report, which has received such a large majority in the committee on agriculture, will be adopted by an overwhelming majority tomorrow. commissioner schreyer, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. first of all, i would like to thank mr harbour very much for taking the initiative with stoa to allow the european parliament to participate in this world conference. we all know that in the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy we have been very active in promoting the internet and the liberalization of telecommunications in europe. europe has taken a leading position in the world in this field. i believe that we too must play our part in the process of globalisation. and that contribution is, of course, first and foremost, to promote free competition. i believe that it is precisely the internet that is finally making it possible for family businesses, small and medium-sized enterprises, to participate in world trade. we have learned that in the field of 'customer to customer', that is, business to consumer and business to business, the internet opens up completely new possibilities. through e-bay and other systems, there are now platforms where even the smallest businesses can sell their products and services on the world market. we have international payment systems, like credit cards, or companies that deal with money transfers. we have international companies that transport the goods from one place to another in the world. europe has a huge need here because of the import pressure we are feeling from different countries - i'm just mentioning china here - which is currently growing at 8 percent, compared to europe, which is growing at only 1 percent. we must therefore do everything possible to make our companies more active in international exports. it is precisely here that a conference like this offers the opportunity to get our act together. it also proves once again that we urgently need to have a parliamentary assembly on the wto. i see that the un and civil society are now engaged in these issues. but we in the legislature are not involved enough in this area. i believe that it will be necessary for the future and that is why i welcome the fact that the european parliament will have a strengthened committee on external trade in the next parliamentary term that we will get involved in this international framework and ensure that the infrastructure we need for our small and medium-sized enterprises in europe is created. finally, we also need far more small and medium-sized enterprises, family businesses, globally, and it is necessary that we create partnerships between small businesses in this world, that we provide them with platforms, that we help our 18 million and soon 22 million small and medium-sized enterprises, which after all have two thirds of the european union's employees in their businesses, to become global players so that we can succeed with them in the world market. (applause from the centre)
